# Changelog

All notable changes to Enhanced Channel Manager are documented in this file.

The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.1.0/), and this project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0.html).

## [Unreleased]

### Security

- **Connection tests are no longer anonymous on an auth-disabled instance that has an operator account (bead `enhancedchannelmanager-2u4e0`, build 0110).** `require_auth: false` is a supported ECM mode, and until now every one of the twelve **Test Connection** endpoints stayed wide open in it: the Dispatcharr, SMTP, Discord and Telegram tests, the Emby, Plex and Jellyfin test-connection routes, the alert-method and M3U-digest test sends, both cloud-target tests, and the DNS-provider test. Each of those reaches the network with credentials your instance has already stored, to a host the caller can often name, and reports the upstream verdict back, so anyone who could open a socket to the instance could spend a secret they never had to learn and read an in-band port scan off the reply. Inside a single router it was self-contradictory: `POST /api/tls/test-dns-provider` would exercise your stored DNS-provider credentials and enumerate your hosted zone for an anonymous caller, while `GET /api/tls/settings`, which shows those same credentials only *masked*, was refused. All twelve now require a real, signed-in admin whenever the instance already holds an operator identity, joining the three identity primitives closed under bead `enhancedchannelmanager-jy006`. **What this costs you:** on an auth-disabled instance that has a user account, a browser that is not signed in now gets `403` from every Test Connection button in Settings. Nothing is permanently lost and `require_auth` does not have to change: browse to `/login`, sign in, and they all work again. An instance that never created a user, the supported headless posture, is unaffected, because the no-identity carve-out still serves it anonymously. `docs/auth_middleware.md` carries the full rule, and a route-level test now asserts the whole enforced set so no member of the family can drift back out of it.

- **A successful password reset no longer writes the account's email address into the log, twice (bead `enhancedchannelmanager-5u5h9`, build 0110).** `POST /api/auth/forgot-password` logged `Password reset email sent to: <address>` in the request handler, and the SMTP helper it called logged the same message about the same event, so one reset put a subscriber's email address into ECM's log two times. That is the log operators paste into GitHub issues, which is the premise bead `enhancedchannelmanager-cb1e1` was filed on when it moved the *failure* branch of this same flow to a user id; the success branch was left alone at the time precisely because changing one line while the other still named the address would have been more confusing, not less. There is now one line for one event, `Password reset email sent for user_id=<id>`, matching the shape every other `[AUTH]` line in the file uses. The duplicate also overstated send counts for anyone reading that line as a metric, and that is fixed with it. The regression test drives the real handler all the way down to the bytes handed to `smtplib`, then asserts the address appears in none of the log records the request emitted, so a future line that leaks it somewhere else in the flow fails too.

- **The password-reset link's host is no longer chosen by whoever asked for the reset (bead `enhancedchannelmanager-qsqfv`, build 0109).** `POST /api/auth/forgot-password` built the emailed link's origin from the `X-Forwarded-Proto` and `X-Forwarded-Host` request headers, falling back to the request's own `Host` header. All three are supplied by the caller and nothing in ECM constrained them: no `TrustedHost` middleware, no allowlist. An unauthenticated caller who knew an operator's email address could therefore have ECM send that operator a genuine reset email, from ECM's own SMTP, carrying a live reset token in a link pointing at the caller's host; one click handed over the account. A new admin-only **Public Base URL** setting (Settings > Email) now supplies that origin. When it is set it is used verbatim and the request headers are ignored entirely. It is validated as a bare `scheme://host[:port]` origin, rejecting a missing scheme, a path, a query string, a fragment, embedded credentials, whitespace and a bad port, and normalized once on save so every consumer reads the same shape. When it is unset the old header-derived behaviour is preserved deliberately, so no existing install's reset email stops working on upgrade, and ECM logs a warning naming the setting and the exposure: once at startup, and once per process afterwards rather than on every request. Tests assert on the bytes handed to the SMTP layer, the message the account actually receives, for both the configured and the unset path.

- **A password-reset email that fails to send no longer writes the live reset token into ECM's log (bead `enhancedchannelmanager-cb1e1`, build 0107).** `POST /api/auth/forgot-password` mints a single-use reset token, stores only a bcrypt hash of it, and emails the account a link carrying the plaintext value. When that send failed, the handler logged the plaintext token next to the account's email address, on the reasoning that an operator might still want it for debugging. The effect was that an ordinary operational failure, an SMTP outage or a wrong SMTP password or a network blip, was enough to hand a working password-reset credential to anyone who can read the log, with no attacker involvement at any point; ECM logs get pasted into GitHub issues routinely, and this project has already imported two such issues containing log excerpts. The failure branch now records the user id and nothing else, in the `user_id=` shape the surrounding `[AUTH]` lines already use. No operator capability is lost with it: an admin can set any account's password directly through `PATCH /api/admin/users/{user_id}`, and `reset_password.py` does the same from the command line when the web UI is not reachable. A regression test drives the real handler down the send-failure branch and asserts that the token it minted appears in none of the log records the request emitted, rather than inspecting the arguments of one logging call, so a future line that leaks the same value somewhere else in the flow fails the test too.

- **A session no longer dies because a token-refresh response never arrived (beads `enhancedchannelmanager-upkp1`, `enhancedchannelmanager-yhk3r`, build 0106).** ECM rotates the refresh token on every call to `POST /api/auth/refresh`. The token being replaced used to stay usable for exactly ten seconds afterwards, which covered two browser tabs racing the same rotation but nothing slower than that. A client that never received the rotated response, because the tab navigated or the request was aborted mid-flight (browser automation does this constantly), was left holding a token the server had already replaced: fine for ten seconds, permanently locked out after, with no way back except logging in again. QA reproduced it in about fifteen seconds, the same request returning 200 at t+1s and 401 at t+13s with the delay as the only variable. The window is now gone rather than widened. The prior refresh token stays acceptable until its successor is actually used, which the server already knows without storing anything new: accepting a successor overwrites the predecessor's hash out of the session row in the same statement, so "valid until the successor is used" is literally "valid until it is overwritten". Presenting the prior token returns a fresh access-token cookie and no refresh cookie, never rotates, and never extends the session's expiry, so a client living on those answers is still sent back through an interactive login when the session's own seven-day lifetime runs out, while a healthy client that rotates keeps sliding forward. Exactly one generation is kept, so a token two rotations old is still refused; logout still kills the current token and its predecessor together; and the token's own signature and expiry are still checked before the database is consulted at all. **Accepted risk, recorded deliberately:** the superseded token's usable lifetime goes from ten seconds to the session's lifetime. An earlier draft of this note justified that by saying every realistic way to obtain the superseded token also yields the current one; a pre-merge review was right that this is not generally true, and the honest version is worth stating because it is what the decision rests on. Anything that reads the cookie jar *continuously* (malware, a shared machine, a backup that keeps being restored) always holds whatever token is current, which is valid for seven days and slides forward on every rotation, so the predecessor adds nothing there. A *one-time* snapshot is different: a jar copied in the moment between a client sending a refresh and that client using the result captures the superseded token without its successor, and under this change that copy stays usable until the successor is used or the session runs out, where before it died ten seconds later. That is a real increase for that one vector. It is accepted against a single-operator, LAN-facing deployment, bounded by the session's own expiry (which this path never extends) and revocable at any time by logging out, which kills the current token and its predecessor together; and it is weighed against a stranding bug that reproduces in fifteen seconds and leaves the operator with no way back except logging in again. The mitigations that were doing the real work (httpOnly, `path=/api/auth`, `samesite=lax`, signature and expiry before any lookup, no expiry extension, logout revoking on either hash) all stay. **A refresh that is refused now says so.** The endpoint previously logged nothing on any path, success or failure: 38 hours of container logs held zero refresh lines, which is why the stranding bug had to be diagnosed from a browser's error body instead of from the server that produced the 401. Every refusal now logs one WARNING carrying a distinct reason code, the client address, a truncated user agent, and the token's `jti` and subject, never the token itself and never its hash. **That last guarantee is about the line, not about the fields ECM picked.** A pre-merge review found the first version of it defeated by the two values the caller supplies: `X-Forwarded-For` was written into the log exactly as sent, with no length limit and no check that it was even an address, and `User-Agent` was cut at eighty characters, which a sha256 hex digest fits inside whole. Anyone who could reach the endpoint, which is to say anyone, could therefore put a refresh token or its hash into ECM's auth log themselves, including into the login-failure lines, which had used the same unchecked header since long before this change. The forwarded address is now parsed as an IP address and re-rendered from the parsed value, so what reaches the log is an address ECM produced or the socket's own peer, never a substring of a header; a header that is not an address is discarded rather than trimmed, because trimming still prints whatever the caller chose. The user agent, which has no shape to check it against, has secret-shaped runs redacted and is reduced to printable ASCII before it is truncated, so it can carry neither a credential nor a line break that would forge a second log entry. The test that missed this asserted the negative over cookie-derived tokens only and now asserts it over both headers on every refresh outcome. The case worth watching, a valid signature presented against no live session, gets a message of its own, because with one generation retained that is the closest thing to a replayed-token signal the server can emit. Since the endpoint is unauthenticated and unrate-limited, repeats are deduped per reason and client for a minute and the skipped count is reported on the next line rather than dropped. An unknown token is refused and logged, never answered with a revocation: one session row is the entire token chain here, so revoking on suspicion would log the operator out of every tab from an endpoint anyone can POST to, and the false-positive cases (a restored tab, a second device from one cookie jar, a copied cookie in a script) are all legitimate and all more likely than the theft it would catch.

- **A rejected form on any ECM endpoint no longer prints what you typed into the log or hands it back in the error (bead `enhancedchannelmanager-9kwzp`, build 0101).** When a request fails validation, ECM returns HTTP 422 and logs the failure. That handler used to log the raw request body, pydantic's per-error `input` echo and `exc.body` at ERROR, and repeat the body in the response, for every path outside `/api/auth` and `/api/tls`. Those two prefixes were the ones an earlier fix had evidence for. A pre-merge review named four more routes carrying secrets in their bodies: `POST /api/settings` (Dispatcharr password and API key, SMTP credentials, Discord webhook, Telegram bot token), `POST`/`PATCH /api/cloud-targets` and `POST`/`PUT /api/sync-targets` (arbitrary `credentials` mappings), and `POST`/`PATCH /api/admin/users` (a plaintext password). Walking the running app for write routes whose request models hold credential-shaped fields put the real figure at sixteen route/model pairs, adding the SMTP, Discord, Telegram, Emby, Plex and Jellyfin connection-test routes and the EPG guide-migration route. Adding sixteen prefixes to the list would have restored exactly the situation that produced them, because an allowlist of credential-bearing paths defaults to leaking for every path nobody remembers to add, silently and with the tests still green. Redacting by field name instead has the same defect one layer down, since a credential whose name nobody anticipated defaults to leaking again. So the list is gone: request-body values are now withheld on every path, with nothing to maintain and nothing to forget. A 422 is not less useful for it. Every validation error still reports which field was wrong, what was wrong with it and the error type, on every route; the log line still reports the body's size and content type, so an empty body is still distinguishable from an unparseable one. The only thing withheld is the echo of the value itself, which for a missing-field error was the whole request body anyway. The test that let this through asserted that the prefix tuple contained the two prefixes, which stayed true while sixteen routes leaked. It is replaced by one that walks the live app for credential-bearing request models and runs the real handler against a real validation failure of each.

- **A failed TLS certificate renewal, and a rejected TLS settings form, no longer write your DNS-provider credentials into the application log (bead `enhancedchannelmanager-2owpi`, build 0101).** `/config/tls_settings.json` holds the DNS-01 ACME credentials in clear so ECM can renew unattended. Bead `2owpi` was filed over that and recalibrated: the file is already mode 0600, is not in git, and is not swept into backups, and encrypting it would put the decryption key in the same directory, so the PO declined encryption and scoped this to the exposure paths nobody had actually checked. Two of them were real. First, `main.py`'s validation-error handler logged the raw request body, `exc.body` and pydantic's per-error `input` echo for every path outside `/api/auth`, and echoed them back in the 422. `POST /api/tls/configure` and `POST /api/tls/test-dns-provider` both take `dns_api_token`, `aws_access_key_id` and `aws_secret_access_key` in the body, so any TLS settings save rejected for an unrelated reason, a bad port or a missing field, printed live credentials at ERROR into the log operators paste into GitHub issues. Redaction was first scoped to a `CREDENTIAL_BEARING_BODY_PREFIXES` constant covering `/api/auth` and `/api/tls`, and it strips the per-error `input` too, because for a missing-field error that value is the entire body. That constant no longer exists; the entry below on bead `enhancedchannelmanager-9kwzp` explains what replaced it and why, and it is the behaviour that ships. Second, an operator-supplied Cloudflare token containing a character illegal in an HTTP header value, a trailing newline being the shape a copy-paste produces, made the HTTP stack raise an exception whose text is `Illegal header value b'Bearer <the whole token>'`. That string was logged verbatim by `POST /api/tls/test-dns-provider`, and on the renewal path it was stored into `last_renewal_error`, which is persisted back into `tls_settings.json` and served by `GET /api/tls/status`, the weakest-gated route in the TLS router. Every error string the DNS providers, the renewal routine and the TLS router derive from an exception now goes through `tls/redaction.py`, which deletes the credential values it is holding by identity before running the existing `redact_secrets` pattern sweep, so redaction no longer depends on guessing a token's charset. `GET /api/tls/settings` was audited and was already correct: all three credential fields are masked to their last four characters, which is unchanged. **Audited clean:** no other route in the router, and no other router, returns a stored TLS credential. **Operator note:** if a renewal has failed on your instance since before this change, the stored error text is redacted on the way out, but treat any credential that appeared in a log you have shared as exposed and rotate it.

- **The credential redactor that keeps secrets out of ECM's logs is now named `redact_secrets`, and the rename is what unblocked the CodeQL security gate on this branch (bead `enhancedchannelmanager-9kwzp`, build 0103).** The TLS credential fix above routes every error string through `tls/redaction.py`, which deletes known credential values by identity and then runs the shared pattern sweep from `cloud_storage/upload_security.py`. That put the sweep's output directly into six log calls, and CodeQL's `py/clear-text-logging-sensitive-data` rule promptly reported all six as HIGH: it decides what is "sensitive data" from names, so a function called `mask_secrets` was assumed to RETURN a secret, and logging its return value read as logging a credential in clear. The alerts were false. The analysis SARIF shows one data-flow path per alert and every path starts at the masking call itself, not at a credential; driving all six log calls with synthetic credentials emits only `***REDACTED***`. Rather than suppress the rule, the sweep now carries a name CodeQL already understands: its heuristic subtracts a list of terms meaning "this data has been rendered non-sensitive", and `redact` is one of them, which is why the neighbouring `redact_secret_values` was never flagged. The rename is behaviour-preserving, it teaches the scanner something true rather than hiding a finding from it, and it keeps working for code written later. `docs/security/codeql-config.md` now documents the technique, and how to tell a name-caused alert from a real one, so the next false positive of this shape does not get suppressed by reflex. **Operator note:** nothing changes at runtime; no log line, API response or stored value differs.

- **`tls_settings.json` file permissions are now probed at every container start, alongside the cloud-backup encryption key (bead `enhancedchannelmanager-2owpi`, extending bead `enhancedchannelmanager-m40pn`, build 0101).** ECM chmods this file to 0600 on every write, so drift comes from outside: a manual edit, a restore, a volume copied without permissions. Nothing noticed. The startup probe `m40pn` built for the Fernet key now has a sibling for the file holding the DNS-01 provider credentials, called from the same startup block. Mode drift is repaired to 0600 and reported at WARNING with `tls_settings_status=mode_repaired`; a repair that fails, or a foreign owner on a non-root process that cannot take ownership, is an ERROR. Ownership is advisory under root, matching `m40pn`, because root reads any file regardless of owner and mode is the control that actually holds. The probe reads metadata only, never the file's contents, and never raises: a TLS configuration permission problem must not take down channel management.

- **Backup artifacts no longer carry the Discord webhook URL or the Telegram chat id, and the two redaction lists that had to agree about that are now one list (bead `enhancedchannelmanager-9kwzp.9`, build 0101).** Bead `9ej7f` made `GET /api/settings` withhold `discord_webhook_url`, `telegram_bot_token` and `telegram_chat_id` from every caller it classifies as non-admin, which deliberately includes the static MCP service principal. The backup read path defeated two thirds of that. `GET /api/backup/create`, `GET /api/backup/export` and `GET /api/backup/saved/{filename}` carry `RequireAdminIfEnabled`, which ADMITS that principal, and the artifact's redaction denylist was a separate hand-maintained tuple that happened to list the bot token and not the other two. The MCP key just refused those values on the settings endpoint could read them out of a standard backup instead. The fix is not the two missing strings. Those alone would leave the class open, so that the next field added to the settings partition would leak the same way. There is now exactly one definition, `config.ADMIN_ONLY_READ_REDACTED_FIELDS`; the settings router and the backup artifact producer both derive from it, a test fails if they ever diverge, and the debug-bundle redactor's private "these two aren't credential-class in the backup contract" extension is gone because that statement was the defect, not a distinction. **Operator note:** a plain (non-passphrase) backup now restores without your Discord webhook URL and Telegram chat id, exactly as it already behaved for the Telegram bot token. The restore preserves whatever the destination already had rather than overwriting it, and reports the fields as needing re-entry. The opt-in passphrase-encrypted artifact still carries them.

- **`POST /api/backup/restore-initial` is now tested against real signed tokens, and its silent dependency on the SQLite pool class is recorded and pinned (bead `enhancedchannelmanager-9kwzp.5`, build 0101).** Two findings from the security review of the `lf29s` branch. First: every existing test of the restore-initial identity gate patched `get_current_user` and `get_token_from_request`, so no test ever presented a real token, and a refactor reading `is_admin` out of the JWT body instead of the users row would have shipped green through the whole suite, which is a privilege escalation to full instance takeover. Thirteen non-mocked cases now mint real tokens via `auth.tokens.create_access_token` and drive the endpoint over a real `Authorization` header, including a differential pair that mints one token and flips only the database row, and a validly-signed token that asserts `is_admin: true` in its own payload and is refused. Second: the endpoint holds an open SQLite read transaction across the restore's `close_db()` → file-overwrite → `init_db()` sequence, which is safe only because the engine uses `poolclass=StaticPool`. Under SQLAlchemy's default `QueuePool` the review reproduced a restore that returns `200` with `integrity_check=ok` while silently reverting to the pre-restore data. That coupling is now documented at both ends and pinned by a test whose failure message points at restore-initial, so moving off `StaticPool` for a concurrency reason cannot quietly break restore.

- **`require_auth: false` now refuses three identity primitives, and what it still permits is written down.** Beads `enhancedchannelmanager-jy006` and `enhancedchannelmanager-ne2yy` (build 0101). `require_auth: false` is a supported ECM operating mode, but what it should still refuse had never been decided, so every admin gate short-circuited to "anonymous is fine" and an anonymous LAN caller on such an instance could replace the entire database, plant a persistent admin-equivalent `mcp_api_key`, and install their own TLS private key. Three surfaces now require an authenticated human admin even in that mode: `POST /api/backup/restore-initial`, `POST`/`DELETE /api/settings/mcp-api-key`, and the ten `/api/tls` certificate/key-material and HTTPS-lifecycle routes. The line is not how destructive a route is. `POST /api/settings` and `POST /api/backup/restore` stay open and both do real damage. The line is durability of the resulting identity: each of the three leaves the caller holding a credential or key that keeps working after authentication is switched back on. Everything else stays open in that mode and is now documented, warnings and known residuals included, in `docs/auth_middleware.md` → "What `require_auth: false` permits". The mechanism is a new `enforce_when_auth_disabled` option on `auth.dependencies.require_admin_if_enabled`, carried by `RequireHumanAdminForServiceCredential` and `RequireHumanAdminForTLSMaterial`; `restore-initial` applies the same rule in its handler because it must also survive a damaged `setup_complete`. All three now share one ownership predicate, `auth.dependencies.instance_has_operator_identity`, which `routers/backup.py` had held a private copy of. **No lockout:** an instance with no operator identity at all (a genuine first run, or a headless auth-disabled deployment that never created a user) still reaches all three anonymously, which is the carve-out bead `lf29s` already shipped and this change reuses rather than reinvents. **Operator note:** browse to `/login` and sign in to use the TLS or MCP settings sections on such an instance; bead `enhancedchannelmanager-p388h` makes that route reachable while `require_auth` is false. Separately, `AUTH_EXEMPT_PATHS`, the entire authentication gate for `/api/*`, is now pinned by a snapshot test whose failure message is the reviewer's checklist, so adding a data route to it can no longer be a one-line, silent, total-exposure change that ships green.

- **The first-run password hint now states the policy ECM enforces, instead of the composition rules it deliberately dropped (bead `enhancedchannelmanager-mkocf`, build 0101).** The setup form, the password-reset form and the change-password modal all promised "At least 8 characters with uppercase, lowercase, and number". `backend/auth/password.py` requires none of that and says so in its own module docstring: ECM follows NIST 800-63B, which recommends against composition rules because they push people toward predictable substitutions such as `Password1!` while adding little entropy. So the first screen a new operator ever sees was telling them to do the thing the policy had deliberately stopped requiring, and it withheld the two rules that actually reject a password, which they could otherwise discover only by being refused. All three surfaces now read "At least 8 characters. Common and previously breached passwords are rejected, and it cannot contain your username", which is exactly what `validate_password()` checks. `docs/user_guide/getting-started/first-run.md` no longer has to warn readers that the on-screen hint is wrong.

- **Operator-shell settings-navigation specs are repointed at the navigation the shell renders, and two specs are added (build 0100).** Four statements in `e2e/operator-shell.spec.ts` still targeted a `Channel default settings` link in the Channel Manager page header. That link was removed with Channel Manager's `settingsLinks` entry (bead `enhancedchannelmanager-mer2o`), so three specs had been failing against a control that no longer exists. No product code changed. `contextual settings links use stable current hashes` now covers the two routes that still render the header's `Related settings` nav, Channel Pipeline and M3U Changes, pinning rendered label, Settings hash, exactly one link per route, and the resulting page heading. The two edit-mode specs move to the primary rail, whose Settings destination drills the rail into its sections and defers navigation while the exit guard is pending; each keeps asserting the exact landing page rather than a bare `#settings`. Two specs are added for arcs that moved off the contextual link. `a deep settings destination exits an emptied edit session without prompting` covers the `exit-and-navigate` decision carrying a settings page, reached by staging a change, letting the guard block a Settings activation, then undoing back to nothing staged. `Keep Editing cancels a deep settings request and leaves the rail drilled in` covers cancelling a pending route that carries a settings page, and pins the post-cancellation rail state: still the Settings sections rather than the primary destinations, with General current because the route never moved. Each spec was proven red without the behavior it claims, by four mutations: `getGuardedRouteDecision` forced to always navigate, Channel Pipeline's contextual link dropped, the settings page dropped from the `exit-and-navigate` branch, and the confirm branch skipped for deep requests.
- **The JWT issuer now honors the configured token lifetimes it had been ignoring, and a refreshed access token stops claiming to belong to `user_<id>` (bead `enhancedchannelmanager-suuoh`, build 0101).** `jwt.access_token_expire_minutes` and `jwt.refresh_token_expire_days` in `auth_settings.json` were already read by three places: the `access_token` and `refresh_token` cookies' `max_age`, the `UserSession.expires_at` written at login and at every rotation, and the `access_token_expires_in` metadata the frontend schedules its proactive refresh from. `create_access_token()` and `create_refresh_token()` read neither, baking in the module constants `ACCESS_TOKEN_EXPIRE_MINUTES = 30` and `REFRESH_TOKEN_EXPIRE_DAYS = 7` instead, so an operator who edited either value got a half-applied change with no warning. Both directions of the refresh mismatch are user-visible. A configured lifetime SHORTER than seven days put `UserSession.expires_at` ahead of the JWT's own `exp`, so `_cleanup_expired_sessions` deleted the row while the cookie still decoded, and the next `POST /api/auth/refresh` answered `401 {"detail":"Session not found or revoked"}` against a token the client had every reason to believe was good. A configured lifetime LONGER than seven days did the reverse, logging the operator out at day seven while the cookie and the settings file both advertised more. Both issuers now read the configured values, falling back to the constants under exactly the conditions `_get_secret_key()` already tolerates (settings module unavailable, or the config directory unreadable) and logging when they do. Separately, `rotate_refresh_token()` minted the post-refresh access token with a fabricated `username` claim of `user_<subject id>`, because it never loaded the user row. That claim is not an authorization input, since `get_current_user()` resolves the caller from `sub`, but `main.py` logs it verbatim as the acting operator in its deprecated-admin-router warning, so every request made after a token refresh was attributed to `user_2` rather than to the account. The refresh handler already holds the real `User`, and now passes the name through. **Operator note:** on a default install nothing changes, because the configured values are the same 30 minutes and 7 days the constants held. The fix matters the moment either value is edited.

- **Agent instructions gain a verification-discipline section, and a wrong claim about stale-base merges is corrected (build 0099).** A retrospective on a run of agent errors found that every judgment error in the session shared one shape: a check ran against something adjacent to the claim, and the claim got reported anyway. `CLAUDE.md` now carries four rules against that. First, smoke-test an instrument on a known-good and a known-bad input before trusting it, since a monitor that fails silently turns absence of evidence into apparent evidence of absence; four monitors failed that way in one session, one of them using a `gh pr checks --json` flag that does not exist. Second, report the check that ran rather than the conclusion drawn, the rule `scripts/check_publish.py` already applies to its own PASS line. Third, never close a bead on the claim that another bead covers it without opening that bead. Fourth, treat your own verification as needing verification too, and prefer uncurated external adversarial review of your judgment, not only of your code. The same change corrects the stale-base bullet in the worktree section, which had claimed a stale-base branch "reverts everything dev gained since the stale base, on merge." That is wrong: a three-way merge takes the stale commit as its merge base and preserves target-side work, confirmed by direct experiment in a throwaway repository. The real hazard is a branch written against an old API surface, whose gates passed against a tree it will not land in, so the corrected text now describes a quietly incomplete fix rather than mass reversion. The standing rule to verify the merge-base before merging is unchanged.

- **`docs/shipping.md` corrected to eight required status checks on `dev`, closing three spots a prior pass missed (build 0098).** `Operator Docs` became the eighth required context shortly before this build; a previous documentation pass fixed `docs/testing.md` and part of `docs/shipping.md` but left three places in `docs/shipping.md` naming the numeral 7 instead of the word: the branch-protection summary, the "one job per name" invariant, and a shell comment above the `gh pr view` polling command, whose jq check-name list was also still missing `Operator Docs` as an entry. A repo-wide sweep for both spellings found two more genuine hits outside documentation scope, `scripts/ci_junit_summary.py` and `scripts/classify_changed_paths.py`, both docstrings, and one in `.github/workflows/build.yml`; all three are code or workflow files and are filed as a follow-up rather than edited here. That follow-up landed the same build: `ci_junit_summary.py` now says six of the eight (the numerator was never seven's, only the denominator moved, since `Operator Docs` runs its six gates on every pull request rather than gating on the changed-path classifier), `build.yml`'s ownership comment now reads two of the eight, and `classify_changed_paths.py` now states `dev` and `main` separately rather than incrementing a shared number, because live branch protection shows `main` requiring a narrower four contexts, not seven or eight.

- **`Operator Docs` is pinned as a branch-protection required context before it becomes one, so the edit that would wedge every pull request fails a test instead (build 0097).** The job hosts six gates: `npm run docs:check`, the em-dash ratchet, the personal-identifier and ECM-credential ratchet, the generic detect-secrets ratchet, the added binary-artifact ratchet, and `mkdocs build --strict`. Four of those are security ratchets and none of them could block a merge, so a failure showed up only as a permanent `UNSTABLE` that a shipper had to notice and refuse to merge past. Two consecutive pull requests on the same day proved the exposure: one tripped the secrets scanner on synthetic credentials, one carried a dead cross-file docs anchor, and both were mergeable. **The reason this needed a test first rather than just a protection change**: branch protection matches a required check by the DISPLAYED job name, and GitHub reports no conclusion at all for a job it skips. A required name that nothing emits therefore does not fail one pull request, it wedges every open one, and protection on `dev` is classic with `enforce_admins=true`, so there is no admin bypass to unstick it. `Operator Docs` is safe to require today because it has no job-level `if:`, no path filter, and only step-level `if: github.event_name == 'pull_request'` conditions on the ratchets, which skip a STEP and leave the JOB reporting. Nothing stopped a later edit from taking that away. **What the contract now refuses**, in `backend/tests/unit/test_classify_changed_paths.py::TestOperatorDocsRequiredContext`: any job-level key outside an allowlist of keys that provably cannot skip, rename, or hollow the job, which covers `if:`, `needs:`, `paths:`, `strategy:` (a matrix renames the context to `Operator Docs (<value>)`, exactly as it does for `CodeQL Analysis` in `build.yml`), a reusable-workflow `uses:` (reports as `Operator Docs / <inner job>`), and `continue-on-error:`; a change to the job key or the displayed `name:`, both pinned because they fail differently and deleting `name:` orphans the context just as surely as renaming it; and any of the six gates ceasing to be invoked by this job, whether it was deleted or moved somewhere that is not the required context, which is the same outcome for a merge gate. Each gate is also checked to be able to fail the job: no `continue-on-error`, and the only step condition permitted is the pull-request-only one the ratchets need because a push has no merge base to diff against. **The gate list is half derived, not fully hardcoded.** The job's own run summary reports one line per gate from that step's `outcome`, so the workflow states its own inventory; the test asserts that inventory equals the pinned table, which means a seventh gate cannot be wired in, and an existing one cannot be dropped, without a deliberate edit recording whether the required check is now trusted to enforce it. `Operator Docs` is also registered in the existing `TestWorkflowContract` required-context list, which independently forbids a second job emitting the same name. **Every failure mode was demonstrated red before the change was accepted**: an added job-level `if:`, `paths:` and `strategy:`; a renamed display name, a renamed job key and a deleted job; a deleted gate, a gate moved into `Backend Tests`, a gate neutered with `continue-on-error`, a narrowed step condition, and an unregistered seventh gate. Nothing the six gates actually do was changed. The `operator-docs` job in `.github/workflows/test.yml` now carries the constraint as a comment, so an editor meets it in the file they are editing rather than in a test failure.
- **Alert-method reads no longer return the Discord webhook URL, the Telegram bot token or the SMTP password, which they had been returning in plaintext to every permitted caller (bead enhancedchannelmanager-9kwzp.13, build 0096).** `GET /api/alert-methods` and `GET /api/alert-methods/{id}` each hand-rolled their response dictionary and put `json.loads(m.config)` into it verbatim, so the whole credential blob went out unmodified. The masking to prevent that already existed and had existed all along: `AlertMethod.to_dict(include_sensitive=False)` in `backend/models.py` substitutes `********` for `password`, `bot_token`, `webhook_url` and `api_key`, and its docstring says "By default, sensitive config values are masked." Neither handler called it. Both do now, and that is the entire behavioural change: `to_dict` is the single masking implementation, reused rather than reimplemented, because a second copy is exactly how the backup redaction denylist and the model's key set would drift apart. **This is what makes the previous build's decision safe.** Build 0095 admitted the MCP service principal to `GET /api/alert-methods` on the PO's reversal, so the shipped `list_alert_methods` tool would keep working, and recorded the plaintext disclosure as an accepted open residual rated P1. That residual is now closed at the response rather than at the gate, so the tool keeps working and the automation credential no longer reads a credential value through it. Every authorization verdict from build 0095 is unchanged. **What a read now discloses, so the fix is not overread**: the id, name, type, enabled flag, severity filters, alert-source filter, timestamps, and every config key that is not in the masking set. For a Telegram method that includes the destination `chat_id`, which is not a credential (posting to it still requires the masked `bot_token`) but is also not nothing, and note bead 9ej7f does withhold `telegram_chat_id` from this same principal on `GET /api/settings`; the two surfaces therefore still disagree about that one field, which is recorded here rather than quietly widened. For an SMTP method it includes the recipient list, which the Settings tab needs to populate the Email alert recipients field. A stored credential that is empty reads back as `null` instead of `********`, preserving the difference between "configured, value withheld" and "not configured". **No caller receives the unmasked form over HTTP**, and that is a deliberate verdict rather than an omission: there is no route, query parameter, header or caller tier that reaches it, none was added, and a parsed source-level assertion over the whole router package now fails if one appears. The legitimate need to prove a credential works is served by `POST /api/alert-methods/{id}/test`, which sends with the stored value without disclosing it. **The other handlers in the router were checked rather than assumed**, since the bead named only the two reads. `POST` returns the abbreviated `{id, name, method_type, enabled}` and never carried `config`; `PATCH` and `DELETE` return `{"success": true}`; `POST /{id}/test` returns the upstream verdict as `{success, message}`; `GET /api/alert-methods/types` returns the static class catalogue, no install data and no stored value. The two reads were the only leaks. **A false comment that helped hide this is corrected rather than deleted.** `backend/routers/backup.py` asserted that its DBAS alert-method redaction denylist "Matches the masking set in AlertMethod.to_dict (models.py) so backup redaction stays in lock-step with the API-response masking already shipped to clients". The final clause was untrue when written, and it is the reason the hole stayed invisible: anyone reasoning about where alert credentials can leak got "the API already masks these" from that comment and stopped. The claim is true as of this build, and the comment now says which part of it used to be false and what makes it true. **Response shape**, for anyone parsing these endpoints: `to_dict` returns one field the hand-rolled dictionaries did not, `updated_at`. Nothing was removed. **Do not echo a read response back into a write.** `********` is not recognized as a sentinel on the write side, so replaying a masked `config` into `PATCH` would overwrite a live credential with that literal. No caller in this repository does that (the one config-writing UI path, the SMTP recipient save, builds a fresh `{to_emails}` object), and the write side was deliberately left alone rather than being taught to strip a placeholder, since a sentinel the writer honours is a second mechanism to keep in step. Related and documented while adjacent: `PATCH /api/alert-methods/{id}` REPLACES `config` wholesale rather than merging into it, so an omitted key is dropped; `validate_config()` rejects a blob missing that type's required fields with `400`, which is what keeps a partial write from silently deleting a webhook URL. The durable half of this work is `backend/tests/routers/test_9kwzp13_alert_method_masking.py`, which pins the property (no credential value in either read body, checked against the raw response text and not only the intended field) against the same key tuple `routers/backup.py` redacts with, so the API-masking surface and the backup-redaction surface cannot drift apart again without a test going red.

- **Five more authorization verdicts from the `RequireAdminIfEnabled` audit, closing two routers that had no gate at all and moving the outbound-policy write and the two DBAS restore paths off a gate that admits the automation credential (beads enhancedchannelmanager-9kwzp.10 and enhancedchannelmanager-9kwzp.12, build 0095).** Read every statement below with the standing condition that all of these gates no-op while `require_auth` is false or setup is incomplete, so none of them can refuse a caller on a first-run or auth-disabled instance. That is a statement about these gates and not a claim that every first-run flow is healthy: the one first-run caller in this set, the backup-destination prompt, writes its already-answered flag before awaiting its request and swallows the result, so a failure of any kind silently retires the prompt with nothing persisted. That predates this build and is tracked separately. **`/api/cloud-targets` and `/api/alert-methods` carried no route-level authorization on any of their CRUD routes**, so any authenticated non-admin account could create a cloud upload destination with its own credentials, or create, change and delete the operator's alert methods, while bead 9kwzp.6 had already put both routers' `/test` verbs behind the human-admin gate: a caller could write a credential-bearing destination and not be allowed to test it. The alert-method half turned out to be sharper on READ than on write, and that is the most consequential finding in this build. `GET /api/alert-methods` and `GET /api/alert-methods/{id}` return each method's `config` blob verbatim with no masking of any kind, and that blob is where the Discord webhook URL, the Telegram bot token and the SMTP password live. Those are the exact three families bead 9ej7f withheld from the MCP service principal on `GET /api/settings` in build 0089, handed out in clear through a second table, which made that redaction largely decorative for anyone who thought to ask the other endpoint. Every route in that router now requires an admin, which is the half of the fix that is unambiguous. The MCP half is split, and the split is a product decision that leaves a known disclosure open, so it is stated here rather than left to be inferred from a dependency name. `POST /api/alert-methods`, `GET /api/alert-methods/{id}`, `PATCH` and `DELETE` carry a new `RequireHumanAdminForNotificationCredential` gate that refuses the automation credential; no MCP tool calls any of those four, so the denial costs nothing. **`GET /api/alert-methods` does NOT, and the automation credential can therefore read every configured method's Discord webhook URL, Telegram bot token and SMTP password in clear through it.** That is the accepted cost of keeping the shipped `list_alert_methods` tool working, which is the operator's inventory of their own alert methods, and it is worth being blunt that it also makes the gate on `GET /api/alert-methods/{id}` disclosure-vacuous against that principal, because the list already returns the same fields for every method. The right fix is the response rather than the gate: `AlertMethod.to_dict(include_sensitive=False)` already builds a masked `config` and neither read handler calls it. That is tracked as bead enhancedchannelmanager-9kwzp.13, it is open and rated P1, and closing it removes this exposure without taking the tool away. Until then, treat the output of `list_alert_methods` as secret; the tool itself prints only names, ids, types and severity filters and deliberately never echoes `config`. `GET /api/alert-methods/types` also stays on the ordinary admin tier, on much stronger grounds: it returns a static catalogue of the method types this build supports and holds no install data at all. No response body changed in this build; this is authorization, not masking. **The two outbound-destination routers, `/api/cloud-targets` and `/api/sync-targets`, are admin-gated end to end and continue to admit the automation credential on every route, writes included.** Sync-target CRUD was already admin-gated on all five routes, so that half was filed as a decision rather than a defect; cloud-target CRUD is where the real defect was, and it is closed. The decision on the automation credential went in favour of keeping capability. Bead jcj0f ships `create`, `update` and `delete` tools for both routers, six tools over exactly the six write routes, and a human-admin gate returned `403` to all six. Managing backup-upload and sync destinations is work the sidecar exists to do, so that capability loss was judged not worth what the denial bought. What it would have bought, recorded so the residual is visible rather than implied: a write to either router names a host, stores the credentials a scheduled background job will authenticate to that host with, and sets the `insecure` flag that turns off TLS verification for that traffic. A cloud target is where the DBAS backup task uploads the operator's archive, and a sync target is the remote instance the sync task pushes configuration to on a timer, with the target's own scheduled task registered as a side effect of creating it, so updating either repoints a flow the operator already configured, and the sync router's own documentation is explicit that the authoritative SSRF check runs at execute time rather than at write time. Encryption at rest and the last-four-characters masking bound disclosure of a stored credential and say nothing about redirection. The reads disclose the destination URL and upload path, the `insecure` flag, the names of the credential keys, each credential's last four characters, the credential version and revocation state, and the outcome of past syncs, which is network topology plus credential fingerprints; what the masking buys is only that no stored secret value can be reconstructed, so a read alone cannot authenticate to the destination. What does hold the line on this surface is that every route requires an admin, that the outbound-policy write below still refuses the automation credential, and that both cloud-target `/test` verbs have refused it since build 0089. Operator-visible consequence, stated plainly: no MCP tool loses access in this build. `list_cloud_targets`, `create_cloud_target`, `update_cloud_target`, `delete_cloud_target`, `list_sync_targets`, `get_sync_target`, `create_sync_target`, `update_sync_target`, `delete_sync_target` and `list_alert_methods` all keep working on an install with authentication enabled; `restore_dbas_backup_saved` newly returns `403` only when called with `confirm_apply=true`, and keeps its documented default preview mode. `test_alert_method` and `test_cloud_target` have been refused since build 0089 and are unchanged here. In the UI, the Alert Methods section of the Settings tab now shows an administrators-only notice to a non-admin instead of a failed request, the Email alert recipients field and its Save button are disabled for a non-admin and no longer fire a request that could only fail, and the Reset Statistics button is disabled for a non-admin; the cloud-target, sync-target and outbound-policy cards were already inside the admin-only Backup and Restore page. Note the predicate for all of those is whether the caller may act as an administrator, which on an install running with authentication turned off means every control stays available, matching the backend gates that no-op in the same state. **`PATCH /api/settings/security` moved off the plain admin tier onto a new `RequireHumanAdminForOutboundPolicy` gate.** It is the only field-specific writer of `ssrf_outbound_mode`, the setting that decides which hosts every outbound path in ECM may reach, so the automation credential could widen the policy from public-only to LAN-friendly. Gating the eleven outbound sinks of beads i4qrp, 9kwzp.6 and 9kwzp.7 while leaving their policy writable by the same principal was a partial control: it could not drive the probe, but it could move the fence the probe was measured against. The always-on half of the denylist, covering link-local and the cloud metadata address, IPv6 ULA, CGNAT and multicast, is not operator-togglable and is unaffected either way. The first-run backup-destination prompt still records the operator's choice, because the gate no-ops before setup completes. **The two DBAS restore endpoints now refuse the automation credential, and they do it differently from each other on purpose.** This looked like a plain inconsistency against the three legacy `/restore*` endpoints and was not; the repository history says so in three commits. `21f93e683` (2026-06-19) shipped `POST /api/backup/restore-dbas` on the ordinary admin tier, at a time when a DBAS restore applied denylist-filtered settings to the Dispatcharr upstream and never touched ECM's own `settings.json`. `e83d31b1` (2026-07-08, bead 6n76m) put the legacy `/restore`, `/restore-saved` and `/restore-yaml` paths behind the human-admin gate because those already did write ECM's settings blob, and deliberately left the DBAS routes alone for exactly that distinction. `fd63235d` (2026-08-04, bead dfkbn item 4) then added `dbas/importers/ecm_settings.py`, a generic attribute-assignment loop over the archive's settings mapping followed by a save, and that is what made the distinction stale. The importer excludes only the live Dispatcharr connection, install-local bookkeeping and redaction sentinels, and therefore not the media-server base URLs, the notification credentials, the auto-creation safety caps or `ssrf_outbound_mode`, so a caller supplying its own artifact could set every admin-only field the field-level gate on `POST /api/settings` refuses it. The gate went stale when the capability grew rather than being wrong when it was written, which is a failure mode the route inventory structurally cannot catch, because nothing about the route changed. That reasoning covers the APPLY and not a preview that writes nothing, so the two routes diverge. `POST /api/backup/restore-dbas` takes a caller-supplied upload, streamed to the config partition and decoded before anything examines `confirm_apply`, and the sidecar has no tool for it because there is no file upload over MCP, so it is refused outright including its dry run. `POST /api/backup/restore-dbas-saved` names an artifact already on the server, which only an admin could have saved there, so it keeps the ordinary admin tier and refuses only the apply: the MCP tool `restore_dbas_backup_saved` continues to work in its documented default mode, a counts-only preview, and returns `403` with a message naming the recovery when called with `confirm_apply=true`. The zero-mutation property of that preview is structural rather than a flag being trusted, because the restore orchestrator forces the dry-run marker whenever `confirm_apply` is false and treats that as the single choke point a caller can never opt out of. **`POST /api/settings/reset-stats` carried no dependency at all and deletes every row of seven statistics tables**, so any authenticated non-admin could wipe the operator's watch, bandwidth, popularity, telemetry and client-connection history. It now carries a new `RequireHumanAdminForStatisticsReset` gate, and the MCP half was decided on its own merits rather than by copying the sibling it was split from: `POST /api/settings/restart-services` kept admitting the automation credential in build 0089 because it rebuilds background services from already-saved settings, work a settings write schedules for itself anyway, so refusing it would have refused a restart the principal can already trigger indirectly. Nothing about a statistics reset is recoverable that way. There is no compensating write, no rollback ledger and no other route that re-derives those tables, which is also what distinguishes it from the Channel Pipeline rollback routes that remain admitted precisely because they are the pipeline's own reversal mechanism. Three new denial messages were added over the same `require_admin_if_enabled` factory the existing gates use, rather than three new mechanisms, for the reason that message is a per-call-site parameter at all: a caller refused on an alert-method write being told it "cannot perform backup restore" or "cannot run connection tests" would send incident triage at a subsystem the route never touches, and the seven messages are now asserted pairwise distinct. A fourth, `RequireHumanAdminForOutboundDestination`, was built for the destination writes and then removed rather than left unused, because a shipped gate constant whose prose says a route is refused is worse than no constant when the route is in fact admitted. The trap this whole epic exists to avoid remains `RequireAdminIfEnabled`, which refuses a non-admin but admits the MCP principal because the auth layer stamps that principal `is_admin=True`, so it closes half a hole while reading as fixed in review. Every gate added here is deliberately coarser than the risk that motivated it, and that is a trade rather than an oversight: a display-name or severity-filter change on an alert method is refused exactly like a credential replacement, and tightening the outbound policy from LAN-friendly to public-only is refused exactly like widening it. Field-level branching is the machinery `POST /api/settings` needs because it mixes admin configuration with per-user display preferences in one blob and cannot be split; none of these routes has that problem, nothing on them is a per-user preference, so a coarse gate costs an automation credential nothing it had a legitimate claim to, fails in the safe direction, and is far cheaper to verify. Every verdict here is proved against the principal it names rather than inferred from a dependency's name: nineteen routes are exercised in four postures each, the DBAS preview/apply split carries six dedicated cases of its own, asserting a witness side effect so a refusal is proved to be the gate answering rather than a handler that happened to fail, and `backend/tests/test_admin_gate_inventory.py` continues to assert both the MCP-denied and MCP-admitted sets exactly, with each entry filed under a group whose comment records why.

- **The whole `/api/tls` router now enforces authorization per route, where it previously enforced none (bead enhancedchannelmanager-9kwzp.11, build 0094).** All thirteen TLS routes carried zero route-level auth dependencies. Everything rested on the global auth middleware, which establishes only that a caller is authenticated: no `/api/tls` path is or ever was auth-exempt, so authentication was always required and authorization never was. The practical effect was that any authenticated NON-ADMIN account, and the static MCP service principal (which the auth layer stamps `is_admin=True` and which the middleware accepts in place of a JWT across the entire `/api/` surface), could upload attacker-supplied certificate and private-key material and have ECM serve it as the instance's transport identity, delete the operator's own certificate with no undo path short of a fresh issuance, rewrite TLS configuration including the DNS-provider credentials that bead 2owpi records as living in plaintext in `/config/tls_settings.json`, exercise those stored credentials against the provider API, drive ACME issuance and renewal, and stop HTTPS termination outright, dropping the instance back to plaintext HTTP on 6100. Three tiers now apply, decided per route rather than blanket-applied, because the router mixes three different risk shapes. Ten routes take a new `RequireHumanAdminForTLSMaterial` gate, which requires an admin AND refuses the MCP principal: `POST /configure`, `/request-cert`, `/complete-challenge`, `/upload-cert`, `/renew`, the `/https/start`, `/https/stop` and `/https/restart` trio, `DELETE /certificate`, and `GET /settings`. That last one is on the denied side deliberately: masked is not absent, since the response discloses the final four characters of the DNS API token and of both AWS credentials plus `dns_zone_id` and the ACME contact address in clear, which is the same class of read bead 9ej7f withheld from this principal on `GET /api/settings`. `POST /test-dns-provider` instead reuses the existing `RequireHumanAdminForOutboundTest`, because it is the credential-validity oracle shape of the eleven sinks closed by beads i4qrp, 9kwzp.6 and 9kwzp.7 and its refusal message should read like theirs. The two remaining reads, `GET /status` and `GET /https/status`, take the ordinary admin tier and continue to admit the automation credential: neither returns credential material, and a certificate's subject, issuer and validity window are served to every TLS client anyway. A fourth denial message was added rather than a fourth mechanism, over the same `require_admin_if_enabled` factory the other three gates already use, for the reason that message is a per-call-site parameter at all: telling a caller refused on a certificate upload that it "cannot perform backup restore", "cannot manage the MCP API key" or "cannot run connection tests" would send incident triage at a subsystem these routes never touch. The trap this bead exists to avoid is `RequireAdminIfEnabled`, which closes the non-admin half of the hole and leaves the MCP half wide open while reading as fixed in review; it has now caught two engineers on this codebase, so the MCP-refused case is asserted per route rather than inferred from a dependency's name. Operators lose nothing: the TLS Certificates settings section is already admin-only in the UI, no first-run or pre-auth path calls any of the thirteen routes (the setup page imports exactly one API function and references TLS nowhere), the MCP sidecar exposes no TLS tool, the HTTPS subprocess proxy forwards no `/api/tls` path, and the renewal scheduler calls the renewal routine in-process rather than over HTTP. Every gate no-ops while `require_auth` is false or setup is incomplete, so auth-disabled and first-run instances are unaffected, and that is pinned for all thirteen routes across all three of those postures. The redaction and round-trip behavior of `GET /settings` and `POST /configure` is untouched: this change is authorization only.

- **CI now fails a pull request that ADDS a credential-bearing or bulk binary artifact to the repository (bead enhancedchannelmanager-6mqn5, build 0093).** This entry is developer/operator-facing only; it has no effect on ECM's running behavior. Build 0092 added `.playwright-mcp/` to `.gitignore` after that directory was found holding 2,321 files totalling 3.1 GB, including `enc-artifact.zip`, an `ECMBKENC`-encrypted ECM backup envelope carrying a live XC provider credential, untracked and unignored in a public repository and one `git add -A` from history. Naming one directory does not close the class: Playwright's upload sandbox refuses any path outside the repository root, so every backup/restore drill round that uploads an artifact through the Restore-from-artifact UI is structurally forced to copy that artifact into the working tree first, and the next such directory has a name nobody has invented yet. `scripts/check_binary_artifacts.py` is the backstop, and it is deliberately not written in terms of `.playwright-mcp/` at all. The three existing ratchets could not have caught this: `check_secrets.py`, `check_pii.py`, and `check_em_dashes.py` all read added LINES, and a ChaCha20-Poly1305 ciphertext has no lines for detect-secrets to report on, so the unit of inspection here is the added FILE. Three rules apply, to files the change adds relative to its merge base and to files merely sitting untracked and unignored in the working tree, which is what the incident actually looked like. First, a leading-byte signature match against ten high-signal container formats (the `ECMBKENC` envelope, ZIP, called out separately as the DBAS artifact shape when the archive carries `manifest.json`, plus gzip, bzip2, xz, zstd, 7-Zip, RAR, tar, and SQLite), which runs regardless of extension because the motivating artifact happened to be named `.zip` while the real risk is the blob under any name; a ZIP renamed to `.png` is still blocked. Second, binary content (a NUL byte in the first 8 KiB) whose suffix is not one of the eleven image and font types a web application genuinely needs. Third, a 2 MiB per-file ceiling and a 25 MiB whole-change ceiling, the latter being the only rule a directory of 2,321 individually unremarkable trace files would trip. The suffix list is a list of TYPES rather than of PATHS on purpose: a path allowlist would red-line every new user-guide section that ships a screenshot, and a guard whose false positives are expensive is a guard that gets turned off. That premise is tested rather than asserted. `backend/tests/unit/test_check_binary_artifacts.py::TestTrackedTreeIsClean` runs the guard across the live output of `git ls-files`, currently 1,861 paths, and requires zero findings, so the 151 tracked PNGs (visual-regression baselines under `e2e/snapshots/`, operator-guide screenshots under `docs/images/`, and the frontend favicons and logo) are proved clean rather than assumed clean, and a newly tracked binary type surfaces as one failing test to triage instead of as a red pull request for whoever added it. The escape hatch is `.binary-artifacts.allowlist` at the repository root: one exact repository-relative path per line with a mandatory `#` reason, shipped empty because the repository needs no exemption today. A checked-in file was chosen because it mirrors `.secrets.baseline`, the nearest existing convention here; the inline-comment conventions (`em-dash-ok`, `fake-test-ok`) cannot apply to a file with no lines to comment on, and a commit-message token like the commit-msg hook's `[no-bead]` scrolls out of view the moment the pull request merges, while the reason for keeping a binary in a repository has to stay readable years later. Globs are rejected, because `*.zip` would re-open the hole in one line. The guard runs in the `Operator Docs` job beside the other three ratchets and reports its own outcome in the run summary, and it runs HEAD's copy of the script rather than the merge base's, so a false-positive fix takes one pull request instead of two; what base-pinning would have bought is bought instead by `TestPolicyIsPinned`, which pins the format list, the suffix list, and both ceilings under the required `Backend Tests` context, so weakening the guard inside its own pull request fails a required check rather than reading as housekeeping. Verified against the real preserved drill artifacts: both were re-planted in the working tree and the guard named `enc-artifact.zip` as an ECMBKENC envelope and `std-artifact.zip` as a DBAS artifact, exiting 1, with the same run exiting 0 once they were removed.

- **Repository hygiene: contained the remaining local scratch artifacts, pinned the backend test gate to the project venv, and recorded three agent-discipline rules (build 0092).** This entry is developer/operator-facing only; it has no effect on ECM's running behavior. `.playwright-mcp/` was never ignored and held 2,321 files totalling 3.1 GB, including an `ECMBKENC`-encrypted backup envelope; a single `git add -A` would have staged it. Three root-level scratch files were also unignored, one of them (`uliyr-post-reload.yml`) exactly the a11y-snapshot class the earlier `h9av3` anchored globs were written to catch and did not. This entry closes the remaining gap in that same sweep: five `.beads/` local tool-runtime artifacts (`backup/`, `embeddeddolt/`, `interactions.jsonl`, `last-touched`, `.auto-import-issues.jsonl`) and a root-level `.coverage` db were still untracked and unignored on every branch; `.beads/.gitignore` and the root `.gitignore` now exclude them, verified against `git ls-files .beads/` so nothing genuinely tracked (`issues.jsonl`, the real issue board) was touched. Separately, the documented backend test gate used ambient `python`, which resolves an older `cryptography` and silently self-skips 9 TLS tests rather than failing, so the run reported success either way; measured `9270 passed / 18 skipped` on ambient versus `9279 passed / 9 skipped` on the project venv. Fixed in `backend/CLAUDE.md`, `docs/pytest_conventions.md`, `docs/testing.md`, and `docs/shipping.md`, the last of which had been contradicting itself by pinning `.venv` for `check_publish.py` but not for pytest. `CLAUDE.md` gained two agent-discipline rules: close every bead your work fulfills (a validity audit closed 46 beads that were already-shipped work never closed, leaving the backlog overstating remaining work by roughly 17 percent), and a third worktree caveat recording that worktrees are branched from `origin/main` rather than `dev`, measured at 858 files behind, with clean status and passing gates hiding it. `docs/pytest_conventions.md` also gained a credential-fixture convention for security tests, because the secrets ratchet deliberately disables the inline `pragma: allowlist secret` that detect-secrets' own output recommends.

- **Minting and revoking the MCP API key are now human-admin operations; the endpoints previously carried no authorization of any kind (bead enhancedchannelmanager-9kwzp.8, build 0091).** `POST /api/settings/mcp-api-key` and `DELETE /api/settings/mcp-api-key` took no parameters and declared no dependency, so the only thing standing between any authenticated caller and the MCP credential was the global `/api/*` authentication middleware, which cares that you are logged in and nothing more. That is privilege escalation rather than a missing admin tier, because the key these endpoints mint is not an ordinary setting: `backend/auth/dependencies.py` builds the MCP service principal with `is_admin=True`, and `backend/main.py` accepts the static key as an alternative to a JWT across the whole `/api/` surface, so an authenticated non-admin could mint itself a credential the rest of the application treats as admin and then use it for everything the admin tier allows. The DELETE half is the mirror image: a one-call denial of service on every sidecar integration on the instance. Both handlers now carry a new `RequireHumanAdminForServiceCredential` dependency. It is a sibling of the `RequireHumanAdminForOutboundTest` gate that build 0089 introduced rather than a reuse of it, and the reason is the 403 body rather than the behavior: nothing on this route reaches the network, so a refusal reading "cannot run connection tests" would send incident triage at a probe that never happens here, which is precisely why `mcp_denial_detail` is a per-call-site parameter. The static MCP principal is refused on top of the non-admin refusal for a reason unlike either group already denied: it would be the bearer of a credential rotating and revoking that same credential. A minted key is disclosed only in the response body, so a holder of a leaked key could mint a successor that survives the operator's own rotation, and a revoke driven by the sidecar is a self-inflicted outage with no operator in the loop. Credential lifecycle belongs to the human operator who owns the credential, which is the same principle `reject_mcp_service_principal_mutation` already applies to the self-mutation auth routes. Setup mode is unaffected and was verified rather than assumed: no first-run path calls either endpoint, `SetupPage.tsx` imports exactly one API function (`completeSetup`), and the sole caller of `generateMCPApiKey` and `revokeMCPApiKey` is `MCPSettingsSection`, rendered from the post-auth Settings tab behind its own `isAdmin` guard, so no operator-visible behavior changes for an admin or for an install running with authentication turned off. `backend/tests/test_admin_gate_inventory.py` records the verdict under a new `_SERVICE_CREDENTIAL_LIFECYCLE` group and pins the classifier against the fourth shipped dependency, so the MCP-denied set is now 16 routes asserted exactly.

- **Every outbound connection test now refuses the MCP service key, and the two admin gates that look interchangeable are pinned apart by a live inventory test (beads enhancedchannelmanager-i4qrp + enhancedchannelmanager-9kwzp.6 + enhancedchannelmanager-9kwzp.7, build 0089).** Eleven endpoints reach the network with operator-supplied or STORED credentials and report the upstream verdict back, which is a status-code oracle and an in-band port scanner, and for the stored-credential members it is a send the caller never had to know a secret to perform: the four in `backend/routers/settings.py` (`POST /api/settings/test`, `/test-smtp`, `/test-discord`, `/test-telegram`), the three media-server probes (`POST /api/settings/emby/test-connection`, `/plex/test-connection`, `/jellyfin/test-connection`), and four more (`POST /api/alert-methods/{method_id}/test`, `POST /api/m3u/digest/test`, `POST /api/cloud-targets/{target_id}/test`, `POST /api/cloud-targets/test`). All eleven now carry a new `RequireHumanAdminForOutboundTest` dependency, built on the existing `reject_mcp_service_principal` path, which admits a human operator admin and refuses the static MCP service principal with `403` and a per-call-site message naming the MCP principal, so incident triage can tell that refusal apart from a plain non-admin one. The subtlety worth writing down for future readers is that `RequireAdminIfEnabled` does NOT refuse the MCP key, because `auth.dependencies._build_mcp_service_principal` sets `is_admin=True`: the three media-server endpoints already carried `RequireAdminIfEnabled` and were still reachable by the automation credential, so reaching for the plain admin gate closes the non-admin half of a hole, leaves the MCP half wide open, and reads as fixed in review. Several of the eleven, including the alert-method and cloud-target tests, carried no route dependency at all beforehand, so any authenticated caller could drive them. `POST /api/settings/restart-services`, which also had no gate, now carries the ordinary admin tier and deliberately still admits the MCP key: it names no host, sends no secret and echoes no upstream status, and it rebuilds background services from already-saved settings, work that a settings write schedules for itself anyway. `backend/tests/test_admin_gate_inventory.py` walks the live FastAPI dependency tree and reads the captured `reject_mcp_service_principal` flag off each gate closure rather than comparing `Depends` identities, then asserts both the MCP-denied set (14 routes) and the MCP-admitted set (79 routes) EXACTLY, each under a group whose comment records why that verdict was reached, so a later change in either direction fails a test instead of silently altering the security contract. Operator-visible consequence, stated plainly: the MCP tools `test_cloud_target` and `test_alert_method` now return `403` and their tool descriptions say so and point at the UI instead, and the "Test" buttons for alert methods and cloud targets return `403` for non-admin users. Admin operators, and any install running with authentication turned off, are unaffected.

- **`GET /api/settings` no longer hands the Discord webhook URL or the Telegram bot token and chat ID to a non-admin or to the MCP service key (bead enhancedchannelmanager-9ej7f, build 0089).** The read path returned `discord_webhook_url`, `telegram_bot_token` and `telegram_chat_id` in clear to any authenticated non-admin and to the MCP service key, while `backend/routers/settings.py::_resolve_settings_admin` already denied that same principal WRITE on those exact three fields. The gate was therefore asymmetric in the direction that matters: a principal trusted to set none of them could read all of them. The read path now depends on the same predicate and returns an EMPTY value for each of the three to a principal the predicate does not treat as admin. Empty redaction was chosen over a masked tail deliberately, and the reason is a write-path interaction rather than a display preference: `SettingsTab` loads these values into text inputs and POSTs them straight back, and `update_settings` writes the webhook literally with no preserve-on-omit, so any non-empty placeholder would have been round-tripped verbatim and would have overwritten a live webhook on the next save by a non-admin. Admins keep literal read and literal write, because blanking a webhook to disable Discord is a real operator action and a preserve-on-omit rule would have taken it away.

- **Channel Pipeline rules now recognize the channels they created on earlier runs (GitHub #801, bead enhancedchannelmanager-0ippw, build 0090).** ECM marked each channel it created as auto-created in memory only; Dispatcharr never stored that marker, so on the next run a rule read its own channels as hand-built, the manual-channel guard rejected them, and the rule re-created its whole set while orphan cleanup deleted the previous one. Every channel ID changed on every run and `if_exists: merge` could never merge across runs. Provenance now comes from the rule's persisted managed-channel ledger, which already survived runs, so a Create Channel action with `if_exists` merge or skip reuses its existing channels and IDs stay stable. Genuinely hand-built channels are still protected: the ledger holds only channels the pipeline created or already managed, never one a rule merely merged into. Event Sync promotion re-adopts its promoted channels through the same ledger, so its internal manual-merge override has been removed, closing a path that could adopt a hand-built channel in the promotion target group and then place it in scope for orphan deletion.

- **The rule analyzer now flags a merge lookup pinned to a group the rule never creates channels in (GitHub #801, bead enhancedchannelmanager-rtst2.1, build 0090).** A rule whose Merge lookup scope names a specific group, while its Create Channel action lands elsewhere, searches a group its channels are never in, so every lookup misses and the rule delete/recreates its whole channel set on every run with orphan action Delete. The new `MERGE_SCOPE_PINNED_TO_OTHER_GROUP` warning names both group IDs and the fix, resolving the destination through the same chain the executor uses and staying silent when a Create Group action makes that destination a runtime decision. It is the mirror of the existing `MERGE_SCOPE_NOT_TARGET_GROUP` advisory, which only ever fires when the scope is off. The rule builder surfaces it while the rule is still a draft.

- **The MCP sidecar can now set a rule's manual-merge option (GitHub #801, bead enhancedchannelmanager-0fn69, build 0090).** `create_auto_creation_rule` and `update_auto_creation_rule` advertised `allow_manual_channel_merge` and the API accepted it, but the sidecar's own endpoint contract omitted it, so the call was rejected before it left the sidecar and the documented workaround for the churn above was unreachable. The field is now declared, and a contract test runs the check in both directions so a field the API accepts can no longer be missing from the contract without either being added or recorded as deliberately not exposed.

- **Normalize-in-group followed by Merge Streams now carries one deterministic target identity between phases (GitHub #845, bead enhancedchannelmanager-ukgj9, build 0088).** Auto-target merge actions ignore stale hidden explicit-lookup fields and consume the firing rule's already computed normalized name. A unique existing or same-run-created channel receives the stream; absent or ambiguous identities remain skipped, without creating duplicate channels or streams or exposing lookup values in ambiguity diagnostics.

- **Cross-instance sync now preserves channel EPG assignments by portable guide identity (GitHub #842, bead enhancedchannelmanager-h3jq2, build 0087).** Sync resolves each source `epg_data_id` to its guide row's normalized `tvg_id`, then resolves that identity independently on the destination instead of sending an instance-local database ID. Unique matches apply to both matched and newly created channels.
